↑ Jump menu📍1 · The Canadians, one layer down

Nineteen institutions with no Asia investment staff on record. The 17 September report gave the allocator, the city and the cheque. This adds the question that decides everything before Asia is even discussed: does this institution buy a fund-of-funds at all. Sorted so the proven fund-of-funds buyers come first.

🟢 buys fund-of-funds🟡 historic only ⚫ no evidence either way🔴 will not buy one F: researched fact with a named source and a date · I: labelled inference

BCI (British Columbia Investment Management Corporation)
Public pension · Victoria
No Asia PE team
🟢
Buys FoF
PE book US$26.00bn · 13% of assets · pace US$3.80bn/yr · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: BCI's own private equity portfolio page lists Asia Alternatives Management among its 57 named external managers. Verified by direct retrieval of the page on 2026-09-19, not by a search snippet. F: Asia Alternatives Management is an Asia private equity fund-of-funds and multi-manager firm, founded 2005, roughly US$15bn in regulatory assets, offices in Hong Kong, Beijing, Shanghai and San Francisco, running primary fund commitments, secondaries and co-investments across Greater China, Japan, Korea, Southeast Asia, India and Australia (asiaalt.com, read 2026-09-19). Its largest fund, AACP VI, closed at US$2.0bn in December 2021. F: BCI's own published Investment Inventory List for private equity as at 31 March 2017 holds at least twelve HarbourVest vehicles, including HarbourVest International Private Equity Partners VI Cayman Asia Pacific Fund; three Pantheon vehicles, including Pantheon Asia Fund II; and six Adams Street vehicles. Extracted from the BCI-published PDF on 2026-09-19. I: this is the decisive finding of the assignment. BCI does not merely tolerate fund-of-funds, it has bought an Asia private equity fund-of-funds and it holds that relationship today. The same fact is the problem, because Asia Alternatives is the incumbent occupying the exact seat Pavilion wants.
Asia PE today. substantial. F: Asia was 11.5% of BCI's private equity portfolio by region at 31 March 2025, against United States 51.2%, Europe 32.7%, Emerging Markets 3.5% and Canada 1.1% (investor presentation, asset class per region table, PDF text extracted 2026-09-19). F: BCI's own portfolio page shows a roughly 13% Asia share and names five Asian portfolio companies, BHC, CAR Inc., Honour Lane Shipping, Navitas and Yinson Production. F: on C$36.1bn of private equity assets, 11.5% is roughly C$4.2bn of Asia private equity. F: the 2017 inventory shows the Asia programme is at least two decades old and was built through both fund-of-funds and single-GP funds. F: the prior 2026-09-17 record notes BCI reclassified China and South Korea out of Asia and into Emerging Markets under the MSCI framework in 2025, so the 11.5% Asia line and the 3.5% Emerging Markets line have to be read together rather than separately. I: this is the opposite of an Asia gap. BCI has a funded, staffed-by-proxy, multi-decade Asia private equity book and an incumbent Asia fund-of-funds manager. Pavilion is selling into an occupied seat, so the pitch has to be a different product rather than a different region.
Named Asia positions. Asia Alternatives Capital Partners programme (Asia Alternatives Management) not published by BCI not published Asia private equity fund-of-funds, primaries plus secondaries plus co-investment · not named by BCI (MBK Partners) not published not published North Asia buyout private equity · not named by BCI (Boyu Capital) not published not published China private equity · not named by BCI (EQT Asia, formerly BPEA) not published not published Asia private equity · not named by BCI (Nexus Point) not published not published Asia private equity · not named by BCI (Hosen Capital) not published not published China private equity · not named by BCI (BGH Capital) not published not published Australia and New Zealand private equity · not named by BCI (RRJ Capital) not published not published Asia private equity · Pantheon Asia Fund II Limited (Pantheon) held at 31 March 2017 not published Asia private equity fund-of-funds, historic holding · HarbourVest International Private Equity Partners VI Cayman Asia Pacific Fund LP (HarbourVest) held at 31 March 2017 not published Asia Pacific private equity fund-of-funds, historic holding · CVC Capital Partners Asia Pacific II, III and IV LP; TPG Asia V and VI LP; Affinity Asia Pacific Fund III LP; Navis Asia Fund VII LP; Orchid Asia Fund VI LP; FountainVest China Growth Capital Fund II LP; India Value Fund V LP; Newbridge Asia IV LP; AsiaVest Opportunities Fund IV LP; Asia Pacific Growth Fund III LP (various) all held at 31 March 2017 not published Asia private equity single-GP funds, historic holdings
Objection. BCI already owns this exposure and is cutting the number of relationships, not adding. It has held an Asia private equity fund-of-funds manager for years in Asia Alternatives, backs at least seven Asian GPs directly, and its new global head has said he will direct a greater share of capital commitments towards a more selective group of core GP partners. A first-time US$1.5bn vehicle under a new Seviora brand is the definition of a non-core relationship. Answer. Do not sell access. Sell the two things BCI is building and cannot build in Asia from Victoria, London and New York. BCI sold C$1.9bn of private equity secondaries in 2025 and launched a Capital Solutions strategy in May 2026, so a direct-secondaries and structured-capital book in Asia is the same work in a region where it has no staff and no local deal flow. Second, the China-exclusion sleeve is a product Asia Alternatives cannot match cleanly from Hong Kong, Beijing and Shanghai, and BCI moved China and Korea into its Emerging Markets classification in 2025, which says it is already pricing China separately. Offer the secondaries and co-investment side as the entry point and let the primaries sleeve sit at 0 and 0 behind it.
Jon Salon
Executive Vice President and Global Head of Private Equity, BCI
Victoria (confirm)
Screened by. No external consultant or OCIO. BCI is itself the in-house manager for 33 public sector and institutional clients, and each client's Statement of Investment Policies and Procedures sets the mandate BCI invests against. Internal oversight runs through a CEO/CIO-chaired Investment Risk Committee and a COO-chaired Enterprise Risk Committee, with the Investment Strategy and Risk department reporting to the CEO/CIO.
Open with. Jon Salon said in February that BCI would put a greater share of commitments behind a smaller group of core GPs, and BCI already covers Asia through Asia Alternatives and a short list of regional managers, so the only question worth twenty minutes is what an Asia secondaries and co-investment book would have to look like to earn a place on that list.
First ask. Go in through the secondaries and co-investment door, not the fund-of-funds door. Ask a shared GP for the introduction rather than approaching cold. EQT Asia, MBK Partners and Boyu Capital all appear on BCI's published external manager list, and a reference from one of them reaches the private equity team faster than an EVP-level cold approach. The meeting to ask for is with the BCI Private Equity professionals who own fund commitments, with Jon Salon copied, framed as an Asia secondaries and structured-capital discussion alongside the Capital Solutions strategy BCI launched in May 2026. Ask two questions on that call: when the pacing plan for the fiscal year beginning 1 April 2027 is set, and how the 2025 reclassification of China and Korea into Emerging Markets changes what a China-excluded Asia sleeve is worth to them. A US$50m to US$100m position is immaterial against a C$36.1bn book, so lead with fit rather than size.
4
Desjardins Group (and Desjardins Global Asset Management)
Cooperative financial group general account / institutional asset manager · Levis and Montreal
No Asia PE team
🟢
Buys FoF
PE book US$1.60bn · 11.0% of assets · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: DGAM launched the DGAM Global Private Equity Fund in January 2026 and makes its initial allocation through a dedicated structured investment vehicle managed by Ardian. Ardian's named principal on the deal is Vladimir Colas, Executive Vice-President and Co-Head of Secondaries and Primaries, so the vehicle is a multi-manager primaries-and-secondaries structure rather than a single-GP fund (desjardins.com news release, read 2026-09-19). DGAM's counterpart is Frederic Angers, Private Markets Managing Director and Chief Investment Officer at DGAM. F: The launch followed the DGAM Global Private Infrastructure Fund and the DGAM Canadian Private Real Estate Fund, so buying external private-markets capability is a pattern at DGAM, not a one-off. F: The pension pocket is entirely externally managed: the Desjardins Group Pension Plan reports 77 investment-manager partners and files its PRI private equity score under the 'Indirect - Private equity' module, scoring 95 (DGPP 2025 Annual Report, pp.17, 30, 32, read 2026-09-19). No named third-party fund-of-funds house (HarbourVest, Pantheon, Adams Street, AlpInvest, Hamilton Lane, StepStone, Northleaf, Kensington, LGT, Partners Group) was found in any Desjardins document retrieved. I: the Ardian vehicle is the decisive precedent; the pension plan buys single-GP funds and co-invests, and a FoF there is unproven. Note the 1 January 2026 change at Capital regional et cooperatif Desjardins, which now makes all of its Quebec-economy investments indirectly through private partner funds managed by Desjardins Capital - a fund-of-funds mechanic, but Quebec-only and irrelevant to an APAC mandate.
Asia PE today. minimal. F: No Asia private-equity fund is named anywhere in the Desjardins public record retrieved. The only geographic Asia number published is the DGPP's total-portfolio geographic exposure at 2025-12-31: Canada 49%, United States 30%, Europe 10%, United Kingdom 4%, Asia and Australia 4%, emerging economies 4% (DGPP 2025 Annual Report p.17). That 4% spans every asset class, including public equity, and is not an Asia private-equity figure. F: the two private-equity partners the report illustrates by name are Insight (international) and CVC Capital Partners (United Kingdom), both non-Asia. F: the private equity portfolio's 2025 result is attributed in part to 'overweighting in Europe compared to the benchmark index' (p.24), which is the opposite of an Asia tilt. F: the DGAM Global Private Equity Fund release makes no mention of Asia. I: Desjardins has no identifiable Asia private-equity manager relationship. Absence of evidence here is itself the finding, and the documents checked are the right ones to find it in.
Named Asia positions. not disclosed (Insight) not disclosed not disclosed Private equity · not disclosed (CVC Capital Partners) not disclosed not disclosed Private equity · DGAM Global Private Equity Fund (Ardian) 2026 not disclosed Private equity fund vehicle, global mandate
Objection. Fee layering on top of a book that is deliberately moving toward co-investment, plus a structural Canada-weighted portfolio that has no Asia slot to fill. Answer. Primaries in this fund carry 0% management fee and 0% carry, so the layer the plan is trying to avoid does not exist on the 50 to 60% of the fund that is primaries; the 2/20 applies only to co-invest, which is the capability the plan says it is building. Pavilion can offer a co-invest-only entry first, which gives DGPP Asian deal flow at fund economics it already accepts without a blind-pool commitment. On geography, 4% Asia and Australia across all asset classes with no named Asia private-equity manager is not coverage, and Ardian's vehicle is a global secondaries-and-primaries platform, not an APAC specialist. On the Quebec point, the fund does not compete with the domestic book: it sits in the 51% of the portfolio already outside Canada, where the plan's own stated purpose is accessing complementary markets, sectors and strategies.
⚠️ Changed since 17 September. F: Three things have changed or are corrections to the 2026-09-17 record. One, the 9/17 note said no head of private equity or private markets was found beneath Nicolas Richard at DGAM. There is one: Frederic Angers, Private Markets Managing Director and Chief Investment Officer at DGAM, named as the DGAM principal on the January 2026 Ardian launch. Two, the 9/17 record treats Desjardins as a general account plus a Quebec direct arm and misses a third pocket that is the actual private-equity LP - the Desjardins Group Pension Plan, C$18.6bn of net assets at 2025-12-31, C$2.2bn of it in private equity, 100% externally managed across 77 partners, with a named Head of Private Equity and Private Debt (Mathieu Goulet), a Vice-President (Frederic Godbout, previously 19 years at CDPQ including Senior Vice-President, Private Markets Funds), a 119% funding ratio and a weekly investment committee. That pocket, not DGAM, is where a US$50m commitment realistically lives. Three, Capital regional et cooperatif Desjardins switched on 1 January 2026 to making all of its Quebec-economy investments indirectly through private partner funds managed by Desjardins Capital. F: no personnel departure was found; Godbout and Goulet both appear in the 2025 annual report published in 2026 and on current profiles. F: the DGPP 2025 Annual Report is the newest primary document and reports as at 2025-12-31.
Nicolas Richard, M.Sc., CFA
President and Chief Executive Officer, Desjardins Global Asset Management (also described as Senior Managing Director and Business Head)
Montreal
Screened by. Investment Management Committee (IMC) of the Desjardins Group Retirement Committee, for the pension pocket. No external investment consultant found for any Desjardins pocket.
Open with. Your 2025 report says the 2026 plan for private equity is to keep building the co-investment portfolio, and your geographic exposure puts Asia and Australia at 4% with no Asia private-equity manager named alongside Insight and CVC - we run Asian co-investment alongside a primaries book and can start you on the co-invest side alone.
First ask. Two asks, in this order. First, a 45-minute introduction to Mathieu Goulet, Head of Private Equity and Private Debt in the FCDQ's DGPP Division in Levis, framed as an Asia co-investment pipeline review rather than a fundraise; the IMC meets weekly, so a manager review does not need a quarterly slot. Ask him for the DGPP investment policy and the IMC terms of reference, which set any staff dollar threshold and are not published. Second, and separately, a meeting with Frederic Angers, Private Markets Managing Director and CIO at DGAM in Montreal, to ask what the APAC allocation inside the Ardian vehicle actually is and whether DGAM intends a second manager on the platform - a DGAM mandate would be a distribution relationship, not a general-account cheque, so keep the two conversations apart. Lead both with co-invest and the China-exclusion sleeve; do not open with a US$50m blind-pool primary ask.
4
Great-West Lifeco / Canada Life (general account)
Insurance general account · Winnipeg (Toronto and London operations)
No Asia PE team
🟢
Buys FoF
PE book US$5.18bn · 2.9% of assets · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: Lifeco does not merely buy fund-of-funds, it part-owns a private-markets multi-manager. Mackenzie Financial Corporation and Great-West Lifeco jointly acquired a 49.9% non-controlling voting interest and a 70% economic interest in Northleaf Capital Partners Ltd through an acquisition vehicle owned 80% by Mackenzie and 20% by Lifeco; announced 17 September 2020 and closed 29 October 2020 (Great-West Lifeco press release, read 2026-09-19). Northleaf was described at the time as a global private markets investments firm and is described in Lifeco's own 2025 Annual Report as 'a global private equity, private credit and infrastructure fund manager'. At the deal, Mackenzie and Lifeco jointly committed a minimum of C$700m over 18 to 24 months across a number of Northleaf's product offerings. F: the 2025 Annual Report related-party note states plainly that 'The Company and its subsidiaries invest in funds and debt instruments managed by Sagard, Power Sustainable, Portage and Northleaf' (p.113 and note 30). F: in May 2026 Power Corporation, Great-West Lifeco and IGM Financial announced a combined US$150m investment in the newly established Sagard AI Fund LP, a closed-end vehicle. I: Lifeco makes commitments to blind-pool, externally managed, multi-manager vehicles as a matter of routine, so there is no governance rule to break here. The constraint is the opposite of the usual one - almost every private-markets commitment on the public record runs to a manager inside the Power Corporation group.
Asia PE today. none found. F: Not one Asia private-equity fund, Asia manager or Asia allocation appears in the Great-West Lifeco 2025 Annual Report. The full PDF was text-extracted and searched for 'Asia', 'China', 'Japan', 'Korea' and 'Singapore'. The only hits are two currency-hedging references to Japanese yen on cross-currency swaps and a Japan line in a tax and investment-income table. There is no Asia operating segment and no Asia line in the privately held stocks table. F: the group-level Asia connection sits at the parent, not at Lifeco: Power Corporation consolidated its 27.8% equity interest in China Asset Management Co., Ltd. under IGM Financial in January 2022, with Power selling its 13.9% stake to Mackenzie for C$1.15bn. ChinaAMC is held by IGM/Mackenzie, a sister company, and is a public-markets asset manager stake, not a Lifeco general-account private-equity holding. I: the general account has no Asia private-equity exposure. This is the cleanest gap of the two names in this pair, and it is documented in a primary filing rather than inferred from silence.
Named Asia positions. not disclosed (Northleaf Capital Partners) not disclosed Minimum C$700m committed jointly by Mackenzie and Lifeco across Northleaf products over 18-24 months from October 2020 Private equity, private credit and infrastructure funds; global but not Asia-specific · Sagard AI Fund LP (Sagard) 2026 US$150m combined across Power Corporation, Great-West Lifeco and IGM Financial Venture capital, artificial intelligence; not Asia-specific
Objection. LICAT capital cost, and a C$10bn Canadian commitment made three days ago that points the marginal dollar somewhere else. Answer. Two things blunt this. First, the 50% factor is avoidable at the margin: section 5.4 charges the fund on the highest-charged asset it is permitted to hold when the documents set no limits, so a Pavilion side letter with explicit written geographic and asset-class limits lets Canada Life apply the weighted-average look-through instead of the worst case, and the China-exclusion sleeve is already the mechanism for writing that limit down. Second, Japan is roughly 30% of the fund and is the largest single country in it; Pavilion should ask Canada Life's capital team to confirm Japan's classification under the guideline's developed-market definition, because a developed-market unlisted holding carries 40% rather than 50%, and the fund's country mix is therefore not uniformly the punitive number. On the C$10bn, Pavilion is not competing for it - that is infrastructure debt and equity in Canada, sitting in the 70% bond and 15% mortgage part of a C$250bn book, while this is a C$7.2bn private-stock book that grew 17% last year and has no Asia in it at all. Offer the co-invest sleeve, where a named underlying company can be capital-modelled up front rather than as a blind pool.
⚠️ Changed since 17 September. F: One material event since 2026-09-17, and it is two days after that date. On 16 September 2026 Canada Life committed C$10bn over five years to Canadian infrastructure and other nation-building projects, spanning power and utilities, transportation, digital infrastructure and other productive assets, announced by Fabrice Morin, President and Chief Executive Officer, Canada Life, Canada. The release makes no mention of OSFI capital relief and no mention of reallocating from other geographies. This changes the room a Pavilion pitch walks into and has to be handled in the first meeting. F: one correction to the 2026-09-17 record, with a primary source. The Northleaf relationship is not from 2024. It was announced on 17 September 2020 and closed on 29 October 2020, per Great-West Lifeco's own close release, which also sets out the 49.9% voting and 70% economic interest and the 80/20 Mackenzie/Lifeco split of the acquisition vehicle. The release further states that Mackenzie and Lifeco have an obligation and right to purchase an additional equity and voting interest 'commencing in approximately five years', which places that window in or around now; no source was found saying it has been exercised. F: two additions the 9/17 record does not carry. David Harney became President and CEO of Great-West Lifeco and Canada Life on 1 July 2025, succeeding Paul Mahon, who stayed on as senior advisor into early 2026. And in May 2026 Power Corporation, Great-West Lifeco and IGM Financial announced a combined US$150m investment in the Sagard AI Fund LP. F: John Melvin remains Chief Investment Officer; no departure found. F: the Great-West Lifeco 2025 Annual Report is the newest primary document and reports as at 2025-12-31.
John Melvin
Chief Investment Officer, Great-West Lifeco
Winnipeg or Toronto (confirm)
Screened by. Investment Committee of the Board of Great-West Lifeco. No external investment consultant, OCIO or gatekeeper is named anywhere in the 2025 Annual Report.
Open with. Your 2025 annual report puts privately held stocks at C$7.2bn across Canada, the United States and Europe with no Asia line at all, and that book grew 17% last year - we would like 30 minutes with John Melvin's team on how an Asian sleeve gets built without the LICAT charge landing at the worst-case 50%.
First ask. Ask for a joint 45-minute session with John Melvin's office and Canada Life's insurance capital team, framed as a LICAT structuring conversation rather than a fundraise, with one deliverable: agree in writing what geographic and asset-class limits in the fund documents would let Canada Life apply the section 5.4 weighted-average look-through instead of the worst-case charge, and get their read on the Japan classification. Bring a draft side letter, not a deck. The realistic entry is a co-invest-only allocation where each underlying company can be capital-modelled before the cheque, sized well below US$50m to start, with the China-exclusion sleeve as the default. Also establish, because no public document names it, who holds the head-of-general-account-alternatives title beneath the CIO. Do not lead with the Northleaf relationship or ChinaAMC; both give Lifeco a ready reason to say the group is covered.
4
Hydro-Quebec Pension Fund
Single-employer corporate pension (Crown utility) · Montreal
No Asia PE team
🟢
Buys FoF
PE book US$1.11bn · 4.8% of assets · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: The entire private-equity programme is executed through interests in external funds. The 2020 annual report states that the Caisse's private-equity investments consist of participations in funds made up mainly of equity or debt of unlisted companies, the majority based in North America and the rest principally in Europe and Asia (Rapport annuel 2020, p.17, read 2026-09-19). No in-house direct or co-investment capability is described anywhere in the document. F: The plan buys pooled commingled vehicles at scale elsewhere too. Its own significant-holdings table lists 3 emerging-market equity funds at C$1,530m, 1 international large-cap fund at C$1,182m, 1 global equity fund at C$907m, 2 emerging-market bond funds at C$551m and 2 Canadian private real-estate funds at C$168m (p.56-58, same document). F: The programme was launched at the start of 2014 and stood at C$1,408m at 31 December 2020 (p.18). I: A secondaries vehicle is a multi-manager structure by construction, so the CVC Secondary Partners commitment already on file puts multi-manager private-market exposure in the book. I: No HarbourVest, Pantheon, Adams Street, AlpInvest, Hamilton Lane, StepStone, Partners Group, Northleaf or Kensington relationship is named in any source retrieved today, but the report discloses pooled funds by count only and never by manager name, so a fund-of-funds relationship could exist and be invisible in the public record.
Asia PE today. minimal. F: Asia private-equity exposure exists, is unnamed and is small. The 2020 report says the private-equity funds are majority North America with the rest principally Europe and Asia (p.17). Inside the private-equity programme the internationally-classified position was C$120m and the emerging-market-classified position C$68m at 31 December 2020, against C$1,408m total. Those two lines carry whatever Asia there is, and each blends Asia with Europe or Latin America. F: Asia is far larger in public equities. Named holdings above C$50m include Taiwan Semiconductor C$170m, Alibaba C$100m, Tencent C$93m, Samsung Electronics C$85m and Keyence C$66m (p.57), plus 3 emerging-market equity funds at C$1,530m. F: Japanese yen exposure of C$414m gross is disclosed in the currency-risk note. F: The reference portfolio also raised emerging-market bonds by 3 points to 7% and emerging-market listed equities by 1 point to 11% (p.23). I: The public-market book shows no policy bar on Asia. The gap is specific to private equity. I: Absence of a named Asia private-equity fund is a disclosure limit as much as a finding, because the report never names any private-equity manager.
Objection. Fee load. A plan that reports total management fees of 0.57% of net assets, states an explicit objective of keeping fees as low as possible, and benchmarks its costs against CEM every year will read 1% and 10% on a fund-of-funds as a second layer stacked on underlying GP economics. Answer. Primaries are 0 and 0, so no second layer applies to the 60% of the fund that buys other managers. The blended 1% and 10% is the all-in number and it prices below a single Asia buyout fund at 2 and 20, which is the alternative route to the same exposure for a plan with no Asia staff. The 8% preferred return means no carry is paid until the plan has its capital back plus 8. Because Hydro-Quebec nets private-market base fees off investment income, the CEM comparison will show the co-invest sleeve at 2 and 20 on 40% of the book and nothing on the rest.
Patrick Raynauld, CFA, FRM, CAIA
General Manager and Chief Investment Officer, Hydro-Quebec Pension Fund
Montreal
Screened by. Comite directeur du Regime de retraite plus the Hydro-Quebec board Audit Committee. Aon is the plan actuary, not an investment consultant.
Open with. Your December 2020 reference portfolio raised the emerging-market private-equity sleeve by a point while the report describes your private-equity funds as majority North America with the rest principally Europe and Asia, and we run that Asia piece as a single dedicated programme rather than as a residual.
First ask. A 30-minute call with Patrick Raynauld's office in Montreal to ask whether the international and emerging-market private-equity sleeves of the reference portfolio can hold one dedicated pan-Asia vehicle, and when the annual Politique de placement update goes to the Audit Committee. Ask on the same call for the encadrements that define transaction authorisation thresholds, which are not public. I: Open at US$25m to US$35m rather than the US$50m preference. The plan writes private-equity commitments into six separate market sleeves off a book of roughly C$1.4bn, so a first ticket at the full preference would be an outsized single position even though the 8% target leaves close to C$1bn of headroom.

UPP (University Pension Plan Ontario)
Multi-employer public pension (jointly sponsored) · Toronto
No Asia PE team
🟢
Buys FoF
PE book US$498m · 4.8% of assets · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: StepStone Group is named an Active external manager in Appendix 4 of UPP's 2025 annual report, the list of 'Top external managers' holding $50 million or more of UPP's assets under management, current as of 31 December 2025 (read 2026-09-19, myupp.ca). StepStone is a multi-manager private-markets house. F: the appendix does not disclose which asset class or vehicle type the StepStone relationship sits in, and the disclosure covers only managers who consented to be named. F: no other multi-manager house appears as a UPP manager. HarbourVest, Pantheon, Adams Street, AlpInvest, Hamilton Lane, Northleaf, Kensington, GCM Grosvenor, LGT, Portfolio Advisors, Mesirow, Siguler Guff and BlackRock PEP are all absent from Appendix 4 and from UPP's public investment activity list. F: Partners Group appears once, as the sponsor UPP co-invested beside on Middle River Power in July 2025, not as a fund manager (myupp.ca). F: Schroders Capital appears via the Real Estate Gateway Fund, a portfolio of Dutch industrial logistics assets, which is a direct-asset fund and not a multi-manager vehicle (myupp.ca). F: the SIPP permits investments made 'directly, indirectly or synthetically' and names pooled funds, trusts and limited partnerships among permitted structures, so nothing in policy blocks a fund-of-funds (SIPP v1.6, effective 1 January 2026, myupp.ca). I: the StepStone line proves UPP's governance can clear a multi-manager house at real size, but it is not proof UPP has bought a blind-pool PE fund-of-funds. Treat UPP as FoF-capable and FoF-unproven until the StepStone mandate is described on a call.
Asia PE today. some. F: UPP has real and recent Asia exposure, and none of it is private equity. Two Asia absolute-return mandates were added in the last fifteen months, RV Capital in September 2025 and Nine Masts in January 2026 (myupp.ca). Two more Asia managers clear the C$50m disclosure floor in the 2025 annual report, PAG Asia Loan Advisors in Asia private credit and Springs Capital (Hong Kong) in China equities (myupp.ca). F: zero Asia or emerging-markets private equity funds appear anywhere in the record. The only PE fund UPP has named since inception is Kohlberg Investors X, a US mid-market manager (myupp.ca). F: the words Asia, Asian, China, Japan, Pacific and emerging market appear in the 141-page 2025 annual report only in the demographics section, in the PAG manager name and in the Springs Capital manager name. I: absence of Asia PE here is a finding, not a gap in disclosure. UPP publishes a complete dated activity list and a named manager list, and Asia is present in three other asset classes, so the omission in private equity is a choice rather than an accident of reporting.
Named Asia positions. Nine Masts Investment Fund (NMIF) (Nine Masts Capital Management) January 2026 not disclosed Absolute return / hedge fund, Asia · Ritchie SP / RV Capital Asia Opportunity Enhanced Return Strategy (RV Capital Management Private Ltd (Singapore)) September 2025 not disclosed Absolute return, Asia macro and credit · Not named; manager-level disclosure only (PAG Asia Loan Advisors Limited) held at 31 December 2025 more than C$50m of UPP AUM Asia private credit · Not named; manager-level disclosure only (Springs Capital (Hong Kong) Limited) held at 31 December 2025 more than C$50m of UPP AUM Public equity, China
Objection. F: UPP will object to paying carry on the co-investment sleeve, because its whole private-markets model is built on getting co-investment for free. It will raise responsible investing second, because a fund-of-funds puts it two layers from the asset and UPP reports against a Climate Action Plan. Answer. Lead with the blend, not the headline. Primaries are 0/0, so 60% of the fund costs UPP nothing at the Pavilion level and it pays only underlying GP fees, which is cheaper than the direct Asia fund programme UPP would otherwise have to build from one Toronto seat. The 2/20 buys sourced co-investment in four markets UPP has no origination in, and UPP can still negotiate its own sidecar rights in a side letter. On responsible investing, point at the China-exclusion sleeve as proof the vehicle already carries client-specific screens, and offer UPP look-through ESG data at the underlying-GP level plus an RI side letter written to its Climate Action Plan reporting. Then close the loop with StepStone. UPP already pays a multi-manager house more than C$50m of AUM, so the governance question is settled and only the terms are open.
Singapore thread. F: UPP already pays a Singapore-headquartered manager. RV Capital Management Private Ltd, founded 2011 and headquartered in Singapore, has run the RV Capital Asia Opportunity Enhanced Return Strategy for UPP through the Ritchie SP vehicle since September 2025 (myupp.ca, manager domicile confirmed at rvcapital.com, article dated 2023-07-09, both read 2026-09-19). I: that is a precedent for a Singapore counterparty clearing UPP's operational and ESG diligence, and it is worth naming in a first email. It is not a Temasek relationship. F: no connection between UPP and Temasek, Seviora Capital, Pavilion Capital, GIC or any Temasek portfolio company appears in the 2025 annual report, the SIPP, the investment activity list or the manager appendix. F: the Ralph Berg move from OMERS to Temasek does not touch UPP on any document read here. F: I did not run a person-by-person alumni check on UPP's investment staff, so an individual prior-employer link to Temasek, GIC or a Singapore institution is untested rather than absent. Jacob Shu, Peter Martin Larsen and Aaron Bennett are the three names to check on LinkedIn before the first meeting.

Four things are new or corrected since the 2026-09-17 record. First, a correction worth making: that record calls TTCPP a single-employer public-sector pension. F: the FY2025 annual report has been published and was approved at the 65th Annual General Meeting, but ttcpp.ca sits behind a JavaScript challenge that returned HTTP 307 to every fetch attempt on 2026-09-19, and no FY2025 copy was reachable on toronto.ca or cdn.ttc.ca. CAAT owns about 22% of its listed equity book in Asia and cannot show a single Asian private equity fund. That is the pitch.
Objection. Private equity is already above target and just cost us the year, so why would we add a new layer of fees on top of it. Answer. The fee objection is the one Pavilion can answer with arithmetic. Primaries carry 0 and 0, so the only layer a CAAT commitment pays at the fund level is the underlying manager's, and the blended 1% and 10% carry sits below what CAAT already pays a single buyout GP. The overweight objection is answered by timing rather than argument. CAAT's target moved from 15% to 17% after the 2024 asset-liability study, the book fell in dollars over 2025, and distributions will pull the actual back toward target through 2026, so the question is what the next dollar buys and not whether there is a dollar today. On the benchmark, an Asia programme measured against MSCI ACWI plus 3% is a diversification argument against a portfolio that is 45.5% United States, and CAAT's own geography chart makes that case for it.

HOOPP is not a house that lacks manager-selection capability; it runs an in-house funds, secondaries and co-investment programme under a dedicated managing director, and the head it just lost came from CPP Investments where she had run funds, secondaries and co-investments. A house that does that work itself is the hardest kind of buyer for a fund-of-funds, because the product duplicates a function it already staffs. The Asia gap is real and the cheque size is right, but the structural fit is weaker than CAAT's, not stronger. I: The realistic entry is co-investment and secondaries rather than a primary FoF commitment.
Asia PE today. none found. F: Not one Asian or emerging-markets private equity fund is named in HOOPP's 2025 Annual Report, its financial statements, or its private equity page (read 2026-09-19). That covers every asset class and is not broken out by class, so it cannot be read as Asian private equity. Asia-Pacific is not being grown. The body is paywalled and I could not read it. A 2018 intention is not evidence about 2026, and the 7% Asia-Pacific figure eight years later suggests it did not happen at scale. I: Absence of evidence here is close to evidence of absence, because HOOPP names managers when it has a relationship it wants to talk about and it named only Radical Ventures.
Objection. We already run funds, secondaries and co-investments in house, and we do not pay a manager to select managers. Answer. Do not sell manager selection to this house. Sell access and pace. Pavilion's primaries carry 0 and 0, so a HOOPP commitment pays no second layer on the part of the product HOOPP could in principle do itself, and the blended 1 and 10 sits below what HOOPP pays a single buyout GP today. What HOOPP cannot replicate without hiring in Tokyo and Seoul is first-look co-investment flow from Japanese and Korean sponsors, and that is what a US$50m-plus ticket buys. On the Canada anchor, the arithmetic is on Pavilion's side: at 7% Asia-Pacific across the whole fund and 49% in Canada, a US$50m to US$100m Asia commitment does not move the home-market number at all. On co-investment economics, open with the co-invest sleeve at the fee level HOOPP already gets elsewhere and hold the 2 and 20 back rather than leading with it.

I: OMERS therefore has an Asia private equity book it cannot add to and no Asia private equity manager relationship to add through.

Objection. Two layers of fees on a commingled blind pool, at a plan that has spent two years taking cost and external structures out of private equity and is currently shrinking the allocation. Answer. The fee stack is 0/0 on primaries and 2/20 only on co-investment, which blends to 1% and 10% carry, so the second layer OMERS is worried about does not exist on the primary book. That is cheaper than the internal cost of rebuilding an Asia origination desk, which OMERS has already decided not to do. The co-investment sleeve moving from 40% to 50% is the same capability Buzanis was hired to grow, and OMERS has already shown it will hand an outside house a discretionary Asia mandate through the 2023 Goldman Sachs Asia-Pacific private credit SMA. Pavilion has operated in Asia since 2012 with a full track record, and the Seviora name is a 2026 parent rebrand rather than a new team.

Canada Post Corporation Pension Plan
Single-employer corporate pension (Crown corporation) · Ottawa
No Asia PE team
🔴
No FoF
PE book US$1.57bn · 6.6% of assets · growing
Fund-of-funds record. F: No fund-of-funds, multi-manager vehicle or OCIO mandate is named in any Canada Post document I retrieved. The plan publishes no manager list and no commitment list. F: The permission is unambiguous, though. SIPP section 7.1 opens 'The following investments may be made either directly, through pooled or mutual funds, or through insurance contracts', and section 7.1.D lists the permitted private equity instruments as 'Private equity limited partnership interests and private equity direct and co-investments'. A fund-of-funds LP fits that definition and needs no policy change. F: The plan actively buys new primary funds. The 2025 Report to Members states under its responsible-investment integration pillar that it 'Conducted ESG due diligence on every new fund allocation made in 2025', and it completed '42 manager ESG engagement calls across public equity, private equity, private debt and real estate'. F: The plan is an Institutional Limited Partners Association participant; it 'Provided feedback to the Institutional Limited Partners Association (ILPA) due diligence climate add-on' and is a member of the ILPA Diversity in Action Initiative. F: Three SIPP constraints only make sense for a fund programme: a commitment to a single private equity limited partnership may not exceed 30% of the limited partnership size, may not exceed 10% of the private equity target allocation, and total commitments to a single Manager may not exceed 30% of the private equity target allocation. I: This is an LP that commits to third-party private equity partnerships every year and has the policy headroom to buy a fund-of-funds. Whether it already owns one is unanswerable from public sources; the Canada Post Pension Plan Private Equity Strategy Report, named in SIPP 7.1.D and not published, is the document that would say.
Asia PE today. none found. F: The words Asia, Japan, China, Korea, India and Singapore do not appear anywhere in the 2025 Report to Members. No private equity fund, manager or geography is named in any Canada Post document I read. F: The only emerging-markets exposure disclosed is public: the asset mix table footnote says 'International equities include emerging markets', and international equities sit at 3.0% actual against a 6.0% target, so the plan is half-weight even in listed EM. F: The SIPP imposes no geographic limit on private equity at all, unlike real estate where foreign exposure is capped at 40-50% of real estate assets with an emphasis on Ontario, Quebec, British Columbia and Alberta. Nothing in policy blocks an Asia commitment. F: One SIPP clause does bear on Asia and is easy to miss. Currency hedging contracts 'can only be permitted for foreign currency hedging back to Canadian Dollar, United States Dollar, Japanese Yen, Pound Sterling, Euro, Swiss Franc and Australian Dollar', with an aggregate FX hedge ratio target of 30% inside a 15% to 45% range. Korean won, renminbi, rupee, Singapore dollar and new Taiwan dollar are not on the permitted list. I: Absence of Asia private equity here is a genuine finding rather than a disclosure gap on geography alone, because the plan discloses asset-class detail willingly and simply has no Asia line to show.
Objection. We run ESG due diligence on every new fund allocation, we are an ILPA Diversity in Action signatory with a net-zero-by-2050 commitment, and we require external managers to disclose EDI policies and targets. A blind-pool fund-of-funds cannot give us look-through to underlying GPs that have not been selected yet, and a Temasek-owned Singapore manager with 20% China is the hardest version of that problem for a federal Crown corporation. Answer. Pavilion can commit contractually to ILPA-format climate and diversity reporting on every underlying GP at selection, and to pass the ILPA due diligence climate add-on through to the portfolio, which is the exact standard Canada Post has been helping to write. The China-exclusion sleeve removes the 20% China weight and with it most of the political exposure a Crown corporation carries. On currency, the fund is US dollar denominated, and the US dollar is on the SIPP's permitted hedging list, so the LP-level exposure the policy actually measures is hedgeable. On fees, the 0/0 primary layer means CEM's benchmarking sees the underlying GP fees only, not a stacked fund-of-funds load.

Irshaad Ahmad was Vice-President, Pension Funds and Chief Investment Officer from February 2021 to September 2022 and then left for Ardevora Asset Management. The successor is MICHAEL BUTERA, Vice-President, Pension Fund and Chief Investment Officer, listed under that exact title in the Officers of the Corporation page of the Canada Post 2025 Annual Report and in the July 2025 Investment Advisory Committee biographical notes, which record him as an IAC member in that role since September 2022. Butera joined Canada Post in 2001 as Director, Investment Research and Policy, was General Manager, External Portfolios, became the Plan's Risk Management Officer in 2010, and chairs the Pension Advisory Council. He is a PIAC and CCGG member. Contacting Irshaad Ahmad would have burned the approach. TWO, THE SIPP HAS BEEN REPLACED. The controlling document is now the Statement of Investment Policies and Procedures approved by the Canada Post Corporation Board of Directors on 25 MARCH 2026, not the 21 May 2025 version cited on 2026-09-17. Comparing it to the 2025 benchmark targets in the Report to Members, the private equity target moved from 8.0% to 7.5%, real estate from 15.0% to 13.5%, and infrastructure from 9.0% to 10.5%. THREE, THE SPONSOR-STRAIN RISK NEEDS SOFTENING. Canada Post reported a record pre-tax loss of $1.57 billion for 2025, but the pension plan itself is in strong surplus: $32.9 billion, 129.0% funded on a going-concern basis with a $7.4 billion surplus, and 109.3% on solvency with a $2.8 billion surplus at 31 December 2025. A corporation losing money does not equal a plan under funding pressure here.

Fund-of-funds record. F: IMCO's published private-equity model is single-GP funds plus co-investments, not multi-manager vehicles. The 2025 annual report describes a balanced approach to investing in funds and direct co-investments, partnering with high-quality GPs, focused on North American and European health care, technology and business services (IMCO 2025 Annual Report p.32, read 2026-09-19). F: The private-equity page names 15 strategic partners: Apax, Ardian, Brookfield, Cinven, EagleTree Capital, GI Partners, Harvest Partners, IK Partners, Kohlberg, Morgan Stanley, Nordic Capital, Peloton, Stone Point, Sumeru and TorQuest (imcoinvest.com private equity page, read 2026-09-19). I: Two of those 15, Ardian and Morgan Stanley, run large multi-manager and fund-of-funds platforms alongside their direct businesses, and IMCO does not disclose which strategy it bought, so a multi-manager position cannot be ruled out from this list. F: The one positive signal is IMCO's own cost note, which states that external management costs include estimated fees from second-layer managers in fund-of-funds structures (2025 Annual Report p.57 footnote 2, read 2026-09-19). That language is methodology boilerplate covering public and private assets and names no vehicle. F: IMCO built out capability to buy in the private-equity secondary market during 2025, which is multi-manager exposure by construction (p.33). I: Verdict note for the brief. A straight fund-of-funds primary is a hard sell at IMCO. The stated model is 1:1 fund commitments to named GPs with no-fee, no-carry co-investment beside them, and no fund-of-funds commitment appears anywhere in the public record. That does not make IMCO a poor prospect outright, because the secondaries and co-investment halves of the Pavilion vehicle match what IMCO says it is building, but the primaries sleeve is the part that will be argued about.
Asia PE today. none found. F: IMCO publishes a private-equity distribution by region as of 31 December 2025 with four lines: United States 73%, Europe 21%, Canada 5%, other regions 1%. Asia does not appear as a line at all, so Asia private equity is at most 1% of an C$11.0bn book, under C$110m, and may be zero (2025 Annual Report p.33, read 2026-09-19). This is the single hardest number in the file. F: Asia exists elsewhere in the house. Asia Pacific is 5% of total AUM of C$90.7bn, 13% of the C$20.9bn public equities book, and 13% of infrastructure (p.9, p.27, p.31). F: No Asia or emerging-market private-equity fund is named anywhere in the report or on the private-equity page. F: The 2025 private-equity narrative points the other way, with C$350m deployed across three new direct investments in Europe, an extensive European market review completed, and an expectation of increasing European manager exposure in 2026 (p.33). I: This corrects the stale undated figure in the 2026-09-17 record that put Asia at about C$5.8bn and 9% of AUM. The audited 2025 report puts Asia Pacific at 5% of C$90.7bn, about C$4.5bn, and none of it visible in private equity.
Objection. Mandate and benchmark. IMCO will say its private-equity mandate is North American and European middle-market buyout in health care, technology and business services, that its co-investments are explicitly no-fee and no-carry, and that the book is benchmarked to the MSCI World Small Cap index, which contains almost no Asia. A pan-Asia fund-of-funds at 1% and 10% fails all three tests at once. Answer. Primaries in the Pavilion vehicle carry 0 and 0, which is the same economics IMCO already pays on the fund half of its own 1:1 model, and carry only applies to the co-invest sleeve. The benchmark objection is a client SAA question rather than a private-equity team question, and the Board Investment Committee's own job description includes reviewing SAA advice to clients. On mandate, IMCO built secondary-market buying capability in 2025 and the vehicle is roughly half co-invest and direct secondaries, so the entry point is the part of the fund IMCO already does rather than the blind-pool primaries.

Intact can size its first commitment below the US$50m preference and treat it as the diligence budget for a region it has no staff to underwrite. The China-exclusion sleeve removes the concentration argument before it is raised.

No commitment to HarbourVest, Pantheon, Adams Street, Northleaf, Hamilton Lane, StepStone, Partners Group, Kensington, LGT or Schroders Capital surfaced. F: What exists instead is an internal multi-manager pooled platform. The PSSPTI and the Teachers' plan trustee signed a master trust agreement with NS Pension as investment manager, and private equity is held through the NS Pension Private Equity Fund, in which PSSP held 76% at 2026-03-31 and 89% at 2025-03-31 (nspssp.ca). F: The statements give the reason for that platform in plain terms, that master trust funds provide access to investment vehicles with minimum investment commitments not typically available to individual plans (same source). F: The plan also holds private equity through a wholly owned subsidiary, PSS Investments AI Inc., at C$323.7m fair value at 2026-03-31, and the statements record that the plan's investments include funds and co-investments (same source). I: this is a buyer of pooled, blind-pool, multi-manager structures that has built the vehicle itself rather than rented one, so the usual structural objection to a fund-of-funds is weaker here than at most Canadian plans; the open question is whether it will pay an external manager for that layer, and nothing retrieved answers it.
Asia PE today. none found. F: No Asia or emerging-markets private equity fund is named in the audited statements, the SIP&G or the annual report. The statements name no external private equity manager at all; fair values for private equities are supplied by the general partner or other external managers and the plan states it does not have access to the underlying information (nspssp.ca, read 2026-09-19). F: The one Asia signal in the record is currency, not private equity. At 2025-03-31 the plan disclosed Japanese yen exposure of C$105,650k unhedged and C$105,596k hedged as a separate line; at 2026-03-31 no yen line appears and the residual Other category is C$37k unhedged against C$(166,051)k hedged (same source, note on currency exposure). Fact of disclosure only; the statements give no reason for the line disappearing, and the master trust restructuring during the year changed how exposures are presented. F: Nothing was found linking the plan to any Asia manager, and no emerging-markets mandate is named. Absence of evidence here carries weight because the audited statements are the document that would show it and they were read in full.
Objection. Benchmark and fee drag. Private equity is measured against MSCI ACWI plus 2%, and a fund-of-funds fee layer on top of underlying GP economics makes that bar harder to clear at the exact moment both trustee boards have marked Investment Services down for missing benchmarks. Answer. Blended fees of 1% and 10% carry, with 0/0 on primaries, put the added layer below what a plan of this size would pay to build the same coverage itself, and NS Pension already carries close to 2% of allocated expense on its own private equity master trust. Against MSCI ACWI plus 2%, the co-investment half of the book is the part that has to carry the return, and it moves from 40% to 50% of the fund. The 8% preferred return means the manager earns nothing until that bar is cleared. The plan can also start on co-investment only, which it already does elsewhere in the portfolio, and add primaries once the benchmark math is tested.

Confidence on the departure is moderate. One trade outlet carries it, Sun Life has published no release naming a successor, and the Sun Life leadership page returned 404 when checked on 2026-09-19. F: The Q2 2026 earnings call of 13 August 2026 has Manjit Singh speaking for Asia and Steve Peacher for SLC Management, with no Asia asset management president appearing in the passages retrieved (theglobeandmail.com). F: SLC Management's scale is now published as C$165bn of the general account and C$260bn of third-party assets across more than 1,400 institutional clients, in a release dated 30 March 2026 (prnewswire.com), which sharpens the competitor reading. F: Crescent closed the largest direct lending fund in its history at US$10.8bn, and Crescent and Pantheon closed a US$3.2bn private credit continuation vehicle, per Kevin Strain on the Q2 2026 call. F: On 4 May 2026 SLGI launched the Granite Index+ target-date funds with private markets exposure limited to private fixed income, commercial mortgages and global direct real estate (sunlife.com). F: New since 17 September on the buy side, the 2025 disclosures do evidence a private equity book. The MD&A prices private equity sensitivity separately for the first time and Note 15.D discloses C$3,396m of third-party managed limited partnership units (sunlife.com, sunlife.com). That is the only finding that cuts toward Pavilion, and it names no fund, no manager and no Asian exposure.
Randolph (Randy) Brown
Chief Investment Officer, Sun Life; member of the SLC Management executive committee
Wellesley Hills, Massachusetts
Screened by. Governance Committee of the Board of Directors, supported at management level by the Investment & Credit Risk Committee and the Corporate Asset Liability Management Committee
Open with. Your 2025 MD&A is the first to price private equity separately from public equity, and the private equity number is the larger of the two, so we would like to understand how that book is sourced today and whether Asia has any part in it.
First ask. A 30-minute call with the general account investment team under Randolph Brown, with one agenda item: how the C$3,396m of third-party managed limited partnership units disclosed in Note 15.D is sourced, who approves a new external fund relationship, and whether any of it is private equity. Ask in the same call who now owns Asia asset management after Benjamin Deng's departure, since that seat is the only route into an Asia-specific conversation. No travel slot. This stays a phone relationship until a private equity line item exists.

Pavilion wins. HarbourVest has had no Asia-dedicated commingled fund in market since December 2022. Pavilion sells the thing that slot was built for, with a written Japan 30 / China 20 / Korea 20 / India-SEA-ANZ 30 split where HIPEP X gives no Asia weight at all. HarbourVest publishes no China-excluded option, so an LP with a China restriction has to negotiate a side letter or buy Pavilion's exclusion sleeve off the shelf. On price, 1 per cent and 10 per cent blended undercuts a conventional fund-of-funds load, and Pavilion is resident in Asia with a Singapore parent rather than running the region from Boston.
Pavilion loses. HarbourVest has been in Asia for over 25 years, has six offices in the region, 233 investment professionals and USD 161bn of assets, and has committed more than USD 29bn across Europe and Asia. Pavilion is a first-time fund with a first close eight quarters out. On most investment committees that comparison ends the meeting, and a Temasek parent does not fix it. HarbourVest is also already on the roster at many of the institutions Pavilion wants: Pennsylvania SERS, CalSTRS, Virginia Retirement System, AP7, BCI, NEST, Norfolk, Shropshire, Lewisham, Tyne and Wear, West Midlands and West Yorkshire all hold a HarbourVest vehicle or mandate. A CIO who wants more Asia can ask the incumbent to build an Asia sleeve inside an existing separate account, which costs one email and no new manager approval. HarbourVest demonstrably does this already, through HIPEP Select Asia I to III and the VSMA1 Asia separate account. The four-year gap cuts both ways: HarbourVest may have stopped selling the commingled Asia fund because Western LPs stopped buying Asia, in which case the orphaned slot Pavilion is aiming at may have been closed deliberately rather than left open.

Pavilion arrives with a live US$1.5bn fund, a first close in 4Q 2026 and a published country split of Japan 30, China 20, Korea 20 and India, Southeast Asia and Australia 30, priced at 1 percent management and 10 percent carry blended over an 8 percent preferred return. Pavilion loses. AIC has sold Japanese private equity to Japanese institutions since 2002, sits inside SMBC and advises GPIF's Japan-focused fund-of-funds mandate alongside Mitsubishi UFJ Trust. On the 30 percent Japan sleeve that is Pavilion's largest single country bet, AIC has twenty-four years of domestic GP relationships and advisory board seats that a Singapore manager cannot claim, and its four named LPs are the exact Japanese institutions Pavilion needs. A Japanese buyer that wants Japan exposure will take AI Capital Japan or AIC Rising Stars Japan 1 in yen and keep the fee inside the SMBC group. Pavilion's Temasek ownership works against it in Tokyo, where Singapore state money reads as foreign and invites questions about political direction, and a US$50m preferred ticket into a first Seviora vehicle is a larger single position than most Japanese corporate pensions will write.

New York, 747 Third Avenue, 21st Floor. PineBridge Investments Asia Limited is in Hong Kong. · founded 2000
Ownership and background
Two owners since December 2025. MetLife Investment Management completed its acquisition of PineBridge Investments from Pacific Century Group on 2025-12-30, and that deal 'excludes PineBridge's private equity funds group business and its joint venture in China'. The private funds group left as Heron View Partners, announced 2025-12-30 with about US$2.5bn under management, 17 people and offices in New York, London, Zurich, Mexico City and Hong Kong. The Part 2A dated 2026 gives the chain: Heron View Partners LLC is wholly owned by Heron View Investments, L.P., whose general partner Bridge Holdings Company Limited is wholly owned by Pacific Century Investments Holdings (Cayman Island) Limited, then Pacific Century Investment Holdings No. 1 Limited, then Chilton Link Limited, wholly owned by Richard Li Tzar Kai. The team ran inside AIG from 2000 to 2009 and inside PineBridge from 2010, after Pacific Century Group bought AIG's asset management group. Huatai-PineBridge Fund Management Co., Ltd remains a related person on the Heron View Form ADV, so the China joint venture sits on the Pacific Century side.

'Management fees for Private Funds that invest in private equity strategies generally are based upon a percentage of the Private Fund's aggregate committed or invested capital ranging from 0.00% to 1.50%.' Pavilion loses. An allocator who held a PineBridge PEP sleeve got Asia inside one global relationship, one contract and one reporting line, and Heron View still sells that shape at 0.00 to 1.50 per cent with a US$25m minimum against Pavilion's US$50m preferred ticket. Temasek ownership also raises a conflict question on Singapore and Southeast Asia deals that a Pacific Century backed manager does not carry.

Pavilion wins. Squadron's LPs are the cleanest orphaned book in this exercise. Pavilion loses. Squadron's history is itself the argument against Pavilion. A trustee who lived through that will ask why a first-time Seviora vehicle raising US$1.5bn with a US$50m preferred ticket ends differently, and Pavilion has no fund track record of its own to answer with. Pavilion's Temasek parent helps here, because a state-owned Singapore holding company is a more durable sponsor than a Hong Kong family office or a listed UK asset manager, and Jodi should lead with that rather than with performance. The counter she will hear is that Asia fund-of-funds fees were the reason these programmes were cut, and 1 percent and 10 percent blended is still a fee layer on top of the underlying GPs.

Pavilion wins. Siguler Guff has no pan-Asia fund an LP can buy. A buyer who wants direct exposure rather than a fee layer will also read Pavilion's 0/0 primaries as a reason to ask why 60% of the fund pays a manager nothing to pick other managers. The hardest number in this record is New Jersey's: Siguler Guff wrote a US$300m emerging-markets separate account at 0.50% and 10% over an 8% hurdle with a 100% fee offset and client veto rights on every deal. Pavilion is asking 1% blended and 10% over the same 8% pref for a commingled fund with no veto, so a buyer who reads that New Jersey memo has a published half-price comparable to quote back.

West Midlands Pension Fund IN UNIVERSE
not named in source · 2022 · annual report
Pavilion wins. Axiom 7 closed at US$1.28bn against a US$1.8bn Form D offering and came in US$502m under Fund VI, so the incumbent's own LP base shrank and several approved Asia fund-of-funds slots are sitting unfilled. AP3 and the State of Michigan Retirement Systems each hold four to six older Axiom vehicles and neither shows Fund 7 on its latest holdings schedule, which is exactly the warm door Pavilion wants. Axiom has taken China down to 5 to 15 percent, so a buyer who still wants a deliberate 20 percent China weight has nowhere to put it inside Axiom, while Pavilion's China-exclusion sleeve serves the buyer on the other side of that question in the same fund. Pavilion's blended 1 percent and 10 percent, with zero fee and zero carry on the primary sleeve, is a hard number to argue against when Axiom publishes no terms at all. Axiom sells one commingled fund and declines separate accounts, so a US$50m-plus buyer who wants a tailored mandate has to look past it, and Pavilion's co-invest sleeve moving from 40 to 50 percent gives that buyer the fee break Axiom's sidecar structure does not.
Pavilion loses. Axiom is on its seventh flagship with a twenty-year record, six prior vintages of realised performance and a named institutional list that includes Montana, the State of Michigan, STRS Ohio, MOSERS, AP3, West Midlands and Caledonia. Pavilion Fund I has none of that, and a first-time vehicle with a 4Q 2026 first close fails most consultant screens on track record before anyone reads the terms. Axiom's founders came out of GIC Special Investments and the firm is partner-owned, which lets Axiom argue that Pavilion's Temasek parent creates a conflict on Singapore and Southeast Asia deals and a political overlay on every allocation decision, and that argument lands hardest with the US public plans that are the deepest part of Axiom's book. Because Axiom publishes no fee terms, Pavilion's 1 and 10 has no public number to beat and the pricing case has to be made blind in the room. An existing Axiom LP re-ups with no new operational due diligence, no new legal spend and no new committee paper, so Pavilion's 12+1+1 term, 8 percent preferred return and US$50m preferred ticket have to overcome a switching cost the incumbent never pays. Axiom's retreat from China to 5 to 15 percent also matches where most 2026 allocators already want to be, so Pavilion's 20 percent China weight reads as risk rather than differentiation to a large part of the buyer set.
Asia Alternatives Management LLC
San Francisco, California (SEC principal office, One Embarcadero Center, 16th Floor). New Jersey's 2016 board memo records the firm's headquarters as Hong Kong, so the firm presents Hong Kong as its Asia base. · founded 2005
Ownership and background
Independent and partner-owned. Founded in 2005 by Melissa Ma, Laure Wang and Rebecca Xu. No outside stake in the management company was found on the public record, and the 2026-07-29 Form ADV shows no parent. The firm itself holds a minority stake in a separate entity, Franklin Templeton Private Equity LLC, a joint venture announced on 10 September 2018 in which Franklin Templeton is the majority owner and Asia Alternatives and the FTPE management team hold minority stakes. The New Jersey 2020 memo records the year founded as 2006, which conflicts with the 2005 date in the firm's own 2015 and 2017 releases.
Overlap with Pavilion: full
🔴
In market
Asia Alternatives Capital Partners VII, LP. Form D filed 2025-10-27 with five parallel vehicles, indefinite offering amount and zero sold at filing. Mergermarket reported a US$900m first close in 4Q25 against a US$800m target for the flagship commingled fund and a US$1.4bn overall target including separate accounts. No press release for Fund VII appears on the firm's own site, where the most recent item is the January 2022 Fund VI close.
Greater China (Mainland China, Taiwan and Hong Kong), Japan, Korea, South East Asia, India and Australia. No published percentage split was found. The firm said in its January 2022 release that China continues to be its largest single country exposure, and that its Japan buyout partnerships have become significant sources of performance in recent years. The co-investment series count tells the same story: China Growth runs to XV and China Venture to N, against Japan Buyout VII and Korea Buyout VI. This is a genuine pan-Asia platform with a China-weighted book, not a China fund wearing an Asia label, but the China weight is the live objection.
Terms: fee Separate-account terms are public in two New Jersey board memos. The 2016 New Jersey Asia Investors III account charged 0.8% of capital commitments on the balanced pool, reduced to 0.5% of invested amount on the earlier of a successor fund or the end of the investment period, no fee on the co-investment pool, blended to an average 0.2% a year. The 2020 New Jersey separate account charged 0.80% stepping to 0.50% on the pro-rata pool, no fee on the overage pool, blended 0.40% stepping to 0.25%. Commingled AACP fund terms were not found on the public record. · carry 2016 New Jersey account: 6.075% on the balanced pool, none on the co-investment pool, blended 3.0375%, with a 100% fee offset. 2020 New Jersey account: 5.85% profit allocation on the pro-rata pool, none on the overage pool, blended 2.925%. · hurdle 8.0% hurdle on the balanced pool in the 2016 account and 8% in the 2020 account.
China: Both doors are open. China is still the largest single country exposure and the China co-investment programme is live, with AACP China Growth Investors XV filing a Form D on 2026-05-14. At the same time the firm now sells a formal ex-China route: it filed Form D on 2025-10-27 for Asia Alternatives Capital Partners VII (Ex-China), LP and AACP Tax-Exempt Investors VII (Ex-China), L.P. as parallel vehicles to the main Fund VII. Carve-out sleeves are an old habit here, since AACP IV Ex-Japan Investors, LP dates from 2014. Mergermarket reported in July 2025 that the firm was selling more than US$800m of fund positions from the earlier vehicles, a portfolio of over 20 China, India, Japan and pan-Asia positions, through Jefferies. · ex-China option yes

Pavilion wins. Asia Alternatives runs a China-weighted book. It told the market in January 2022 that China remains its largest single country exposure, and its co-investment series has reached AACP China Growth Investors XV while Japan Buyout stops at VII. Pavilion sells Japan 30 and China 20, which is the shape most 2026 investment committees are actually asking for. Pavilion's Temasek parent is an asset in Asia itself, because it buys Singapore and regional general partner access that a San Francisco adviser has to earn with travel. The competitor is also shrinking. Regulatory AUM fell from about US$16.5bn to US$13.24bn, it sold more than US$800m of fund positions through Jefferies in 2025, and it has published nothing at all about Fund VII. An LP who wants a manager with room to grow has a reason to hear Pavilion out. Pavilion's co-investment weighting of 40 moving to 50 is also higher than the pro-rata pools Asia Alternatives writes into its separate accounts.
Pavilion loses. Pavilion loses this one on price and on record. New Jersey's own board papers put the Asia Alternatives separate account at a blended 0.40% management fee stepping to 0.25%, a blended 2.925% profit allocation and an 8% hurdle, with no fee and no carry at all on the overage pool. The 2016 account was cheaper still at a blended 0.2% and 3.04%. Pavilion asks 1% and 10% blended on the same 8% preferred return. A US$50m ticket buyer puts those two numbers side by side and Pavilion is several times the fee and three times the carry, and the buyer will say so out loud. Record is the second problem. Asia Alternatives is on its seventh flagship with a first vintage of 2007, and Pennsylvania SERS has funded six separate Asia Alternatives vehicles since June 2007 and still holds them. Pavilion Fund I has no realised Asia fund-of-funds track record of its own and a first close that has not happened yet. Temasek ownership cuts against Pavilion here. A US state fund that is already trimming China will ask harder questions of a Singapore sovereign wealth subsidiary than of a partner-owned US registered adviser, and Asia Alternatives is the one with the clean answer on that point.
Roc Partners
Sydney · founded 2014
Ownership and background
Management owned since the June 2014 buy-out of Macquarie Group's private markets business, previously Macquarie Investment Management Private Markets, with the business operating since 1996. Not wholly independent: the ASX-listed Pacific Current Group raised its stake in Roc Partners Pty Limited from 18 per cent to 30 per cent for A$6.8m in July 2019 and still carries Roc in its boutique portfolio. Roc made a non-binding takeover proposal for Pacific Current in May 2026 which Pacific Current declined on 7 September 2026.

Pavilion wins. Roc's Asia-Pacific product is small and Australian centred. The whole US feeder for Fund VI is US$300m, so Pavilion's preferred US$50m ticket would be a sixth of it and a large institution cannot size a position there. Pavilion offers a stated Japan 30, China 20 and Korea 20 weighting where Roc publishes no country mix at all, and Temasek ownership buys North Asian manager access that a Sydney house with two China offices does not match. This is a commercial pan-Asia competitor rather than a domestic policy fund, and on fee optics Pavilion's 1% and 10% blended terms are a live argument because Roc publishes nothing.
Pavilion loses. Roc has been investing since 1996, has made more than 150 fund commitments and 600 investments, and runs about AU$9.5bn as at 30 September 2025. A sixth numbered Asia-Pacific fund with that history beats a first Seviora PavCap vehicle on almost any investment committee scorecard. Roc also takes US$5m minimums, so it serves mid-sized buyers that Pavilion's US$50m preference turns away. Temasek ownership cuts against Pavilion with Australian super funds, which run their own state-ownership and China-exposure screens and already have a local Asia-Pacific manager on the panel.
LGT Capital Partners
Pfaeffikon SZ, Switzerland · founded 1997
Ownership and background
F: subsidiary of LGT Group, which is owned by the Princely House of Liechtenstein. LGT Group was founded in 1920 and had CHF 386.1bn of assets under management at 31 December 2025. LGT Capital Partners states 16 offices and over 950 professionals, and serves more than 700 institutional clients in over 40 countries. I: the owner is a single family office balance sheet rather than a listed parent or a state holding company, so LGT invests its own money alongside clients and has no quarterly earnings pressure on fundraising. No Seoul office: the Asian offices are Beijing, Hong Kong, Singapore, Sydney and Tokyo. 1997 is the year of the first private equity investment, stated on LGT's own private markets page.
Overlap with Pavilion: full
🔴
In market
Crown Asia-Pacific Private Equity VI, in market, USD 1.2bn target, USD 681.6m sold across 43 investors as at 4 September 2025
F: no published country weights. LGT argues against fixed weights: a successful APAC portfolio needs 'a balanced and diversified approach, without overweighting any particular geography or segment', and the region should be treated as a 'menu of opportunities'. F: LGT's July 2025 APAC whitepaper covers Japan, Korea, Australia and New Zealand, India, Southeast Asia and Greater China as separate country theses. F: market-wide, China's share of capital deployed in Asia PE fell from 47% in 2010-2015 and 46% in 2016-2021 to 30% in 2022-2024 (AVCJ data to 31 December 2024, reproduced by LGT). F: LGT targets small and mid-market managers: 'Since 2020, the median fund size we have committed to via our flagship APAC fund is approximately USD 550 million, with the fund size being USD 2 billion or less in 90% of cases'. F: 'Having allocated to the region for over 25 years' and present in Asia-Pacific since 1998. I: a true pan-Asia product, not a China fund with an Asia label, but one that refuses to commit to a country split in writing. Pavilion's published Japan 30 / China 20 / Korea 20 / India-SEA-ANZ 30 is a contractual answer where LGT offers manager discretion.
China: F: LGT stays in China and says so in print. Pull-quote from its July 2025 APAC whitepaper: 'China remains one of the most interesting private equity markets in APAC in the long term'. The paper closes 'now is not the time to pull out of APAC', notes 'a great deal of China risk is already priced in across public and private equity markets', and argues that 'compelling opportunities remain for buyers of certain Chinese assets'. F: no China-excluded or ex-China share class, sleeve or parallel vehicle is published for any Crown Asia-Pacific fund. I: LGT is the clearest doubling-down case in the peer set, which is a direct opening for Pavilion with any LP that carries a China restriction. · ex-China option not found

Achmea Investment Management B.V. IN UNIVERSE

Pavilion wins. LGT publishes no China-excluded option and argues in print for staying in China, so an LP with a China restriction cannot buy Crown Asia-Pacific off the shelf and can buy Pavilion's China-exclusion sleeve. LGT also refuses to commit to a country split, telling investors to treat the region as a menu of opportunities; Pavilion sells a written Japan 30 / China 20 / Korea 20 / India-SEA-ANZ 30 allocation, which is an answer a trustee can put in a minute. Pavilion is resident in Asia with a Temasek parent, where LGT runs the region from Hong Kong under a Swiss head office. Pavilion's 1 per cent management fee and 10 per cent blended carry is below a conventional fund-of-funds load, and LGT's terms are not public, so the fee question is worth forcing into the open in every competitive process.
Pavilion loses. LGT is on its sixth Asia fund and has been investing in Asia-Pacific since 1998. CAPE V closed at its USD 1.65bn hard cap against a USD 1bn target with more than 50 institutions across Europe, the US, Canada, Australia and the Middle East. Pavilion is a first-time fund with a first close eight quarters away. On most investment committees a sixth fund with a 28-year regional record wins that comparison, and the Temasek parent does not fix it. LGT also sells through Irish plc and Luxembourg SICAV-RAIF feeders that European institutions already hold, so the operational and tax review is already done; a Singapore structure is a fresh piece of work for the same committee. Worse, LGT sits in front of the money in places Pavilion wants to reach: it has held the private equity selection screen at Achmea IM since March 2026 and runs three Keva-dedicated emerging-Asia vehicles, so in those accounts Pavilion is pitching its competitor rather than the LP. Temasek ownership cuts both ways, because Temasek is itself an active LP and direct investor across Asia and some LPs will read that as a conflict rather than an edge.

Pavilion wins. Hamilton Lane sells no commingled closed-end Asia private-equity fund. An institution that wants a discrete, sized, drawdown Asia allocation cannot buy one from Hamilton Lane; it gets Asia inside a global separate account at whatever weight Hamilton Lane chooses, or it buys an evergreen with monthly dealing. Pavilion sells a published country split, Japan 30, China 20, Korea 20, India-SEA-ANZ 30, that a board can approve and measure. The Asia evergreen is directs and secondaries only, so an LP that wants primary access to Asian GPs gets nothing from it, and Pavilion's 60-to-50 primaries sleeve is the whole point of the fund.
Pavilion loses. Hamilton Lane holds USD 92bn of customized separate accounts and is already the private-equity gatekeeper at Guardian Life, Fresno County, PERSI and Illinois State Board of Investment. At those desks the Asia decision is Hamilton Lane's, and it can add Asia inside a mandate the client already pays for rather than approving a second manager at 1% and 10%. Against a 1991 firm with USD 142bn of discretionary AUM, USD 905bn of advisory assets, quarterly NASDAQ reporting and a published 20-year fund-by-fund track record, Pavilion is a first commingled fund from a manager whose parent renamed itself in February 2026. On most investment committees that comparison decides the meeting before fees are discussed.

Chicago, Illinois, United States · founded 1972

F: employee-owned and 100% independent since 2001. F: the firm marked 50 years in private markets in 2022, so the 1972 founding date is its own. In market
Adams Street Asia Fund II LP, incorporated 2023, first sale 11 July 2024, USD 90.1m sold to 4 investors as at the Form D/A of 28 August 2026. It is still raising.

F: no separate China-excluded share class is published, because the Asia primary practice is ex-China by construction rather than by option. I: an LP that wants Asia without China can buy Adams Street Asia Fund II today and does not need a sleeve, a side letter or a new manager. That is a direct competitive threat to the single feature Pavilion is using to differentiate. Pavilion wins. Size and structure. Adams Street Asia Fund II has sold USD 90.1m to four investors after two years in market, and its predecessor raised USD 100.4m from six. Adams Street's Asia funds are primaries-led with no Asia secondaries vehicle, which leaves Pavilion's direct-secondaries sleeve uncontested at this manager.
Pavilion loses. The brief's hypothesis was that Adams Street sells no Asia-dedicated product. That is wrong, and the correction is the finding. That is Pavilion's China-exclusion sleeve, already built, already funded, from a 1972 firm with USD 76bn, employee ownership, six Asia-Pacific offices, a Singapore-based partner who has led Asia primaries for years, and an Asia research programme running since 1999. An LP choosing between an ex-China Asia fund from Adams Street and one from a first-time Temasek-owned vehicle closing in 4Q 2026 has an easy answer on track record and on domicile. Every UK and Canadian institution evidenced above already holds Adams Street globally, so the incumbency is in place too. Pavilion's counter is scale and breadth, not credibility.

Pavilion wins. StepStone's only Asia private-equity vehicle is a separate account built for one pension fund.
