Pavilion loses. StepStone is the private-equity consultant at NYSTRS and the alternatives consultant at DCRB, and it writes the pacing plan those boards approve. Pavilion has to sell past a gatekeeper that can deliver the same Asia exposure inside an account it already runs, at SMA pricing well below 1% and 10%. With USD 233bn of AUM, USD 885bn of total capital under oversight, offices in Beijing, Hong Kong, Seoul, Tokyo and Singapore and nineteen years of history, StepStone can answer the Asia question without a new manager, a new legal negotiation or a new line on the board report. That is the argument that ends a first-time-fund pitch.

China: Stepped back from China and rotated into India and South East Asia. Mergermarket reported in September 2024 that TR's most recent China deal had closed about two years earlier and quoted chief executive Paul Robine saying the firm wants to see how the exit environment evolves before committing again. The 2025 news record is dominated by China exits, including full exits from WeRide, NetEase Cloud Music and Full Truck Alliance in April 2025 and Source Photonics in September 2025. The firm had been named a top-10 China secondaries investor by Zero2IPO in 2021 and 2022, so this is a deliberate reversal. No China-excluded share class or ex-China vehicle found. · ex-China option not found
No named investor found on the public record.
Pavilion wins. TR Capital raised US$401m for Fund V against a US$500m to US$600m target, so a US$50m Pavilion ticket would be a far larger share of TR's fund than of a US$1.5bn vehicle, and concentration alone rules TR out for some allocators. TR runs no primaries and no multi-manager book, so it cannot serve an LP that wants one Asia relationship covering funds, co-investment and secondaries together. Its portfolio carries no Japan and no Korea, which leaves Pavilion's Japan 30 and Korea 20 weightings uncontested. Pavilion's blended 1% and 10% with an 8% preferred return prices below a standard secondaries fee load.
Pavilion loses. TR Capital has been pricing Asian single-asset secondaries since 2007, is on its fifth fund and reported a 92% re-up rate from existing LPs on Fund V, which is a harder retention number to argue against than a first close scheduled for 4Q 2026. It has 35 people across seven offices, including Shanghai, Shenzhen, Mumbai and Bangalore, and it sources bilaterally from sponsors it has transacted with for a decade. A buyer that wants Asia secondaries alone gets a specialist there and would get a 50 to 60 percent primaries allocation at Pavilion. Temasek ownership also counts against Pavilion with Chinese and Indian sellers who prefer a counterparty with no state shareholder.
Northleaf Capital Partners
Toronto, Canada · founded 2009
Ownership and background
Employee-controlled and operationally independent. Mackenzie Investments, a subsidiary of IGM Financial, and Great-West Lifeco hold a 49.9 per cent non-controlling voting interest and a 70 per cent economic interest through an acquisition vehicle owned 80 per cent by Mackenzie and 20 per cent by Lifeco. Northleaf's senior leadership keeps 50.1 per cent of the votes: 'Northleaf's senior leadership will maintain a 50.1 per cent voting interest in the firm, which will remain employee-controlled and operationally independent.' Announced 2020-09-17, closed 2020-10-29. IGM Financial and Great-West Lifeco are both members of the Power Corporation of Canada group. At announcement, 'IGM and Lifeco will initially commit a minimum of $700 million across a number of Northleaf's private equity, private credit and infrastructure funds'. Northleaf spun out of TD Bank in 2009; the predecessor TD Capital launched its fund investment programme in 1969.
Overlap with Pavilion: global FoF with Asia sleeve
⚫
Not a peer
Northleaf Secondary Partners (US) IV LP, US$776.5m sold as of the Form D/A dated 2026-07-08.
Global developed markets, mid-market. GP relationships across North America, Europe and Asia-Pacific. No published country split, no Asia target weight, and the programme is described as 'an investor in private equity, private credit and infrastructure in developed markets worldwide'.
China: Not published. Northleaf sells a developed-markets programme, which leaves China out by construction rather than by a stated China policy. No China, ex-China or China-exclusion language appears on the firm site or in the Form ADV. · ex-China option not found
No named investor found on the public record.
Pavilion wins. Northleaf sells a global developed-markets mid-market programme, and its own milestone page files the 1996 first Asia fund investment under 'exposure to investments in developed markets around the world'. An allocator who wants a stated Japan 30, China 20, Korea 20 and India-SEA-ANZ 30 weighting cannot buy it from Northleaf at any price. Pavilion underwrites Asian general partners from Singapore with regional staff, and prices at 1 per cent and 10 per cent blended with an 8 per cent preferred return against Northleaf terms that are not published at all.
Pavilion loses. Northleaf has raised more than US$30bn from 375 investors across eight private equity funds since 2002, and Mackenzie and Great-West Lifeco bought 70 per cent of the economics in 2020, so an investment committee's manager risk question is already answered. Pavilion arrives with a 4Q 2026 first close, a US$50m preferred ticket and no track record under the Seviora name. Northleaf has sold from Tokyo since 2022 and Seoul since 2024, so it already sits in front of the Japanese and Korean institutions Pavilion needs, and a Canadian owner reads as neutral where Temasek ownership of Pavilion's parent raises a conflict question on Singapore and Southeast Asia deals.

Newton, Massachusetts, United States · founded 1998

Independent. No parent or outside owner is disclosed on the Form ADV or the firm site. SEC-registered investment adviser 801-60272, CRD 111497. Prior office was One Newton Executive Park, 2221 Washington Street, Newton.
Overlap with Pavilion: global FoF with Asia sleeve

Not a peer
Keva Investments VI, L.P., gross asset value US$80,553,015 on the Form ADV filed 2026-05-12.
No published country split and no Asia weight. The offer page says GroveStreet backs 'leading IT and life science venture teams in the US, Europe and Asia' and 'buyout teams focused on the lower mid-market in the US and Europe as well as growth equity teams in Asia'. Neither Form ADV part names a geography at all.
Terms: fee Negotiated per client, no schedule published. 'All GroveStreet fees are subject to negotiation. GroveStreet is not required to provide a fee schedule as all investors are qualified purchasers.' Payable quarterly in advance. Programmes generally start at US$100m of commitments and the firm has accepted smaller. · carry Carried interest on every Program, allocated to the relevant general partner entity on achieving a negotiated Hurdle Rate, with distribution delayed until the investor has been returned no less than 100 per cent of contributed capital. · hurdle A negotiated Hurdle Rate applies to every Program. Pavilion wins. GroveStreet has no Asia product to sell. Forty-two private funds on the 2026 Form ADV and a Part 2A brochure with zero mentions of Asia means an allocator who wants Asia has to commission a bespoke mandate and pay GroveStreet to build a team and a GP network from scratch. Programmes generally start at US$100m, twice Pavilion's US$50m preferred ticket, and GroveStreet takes carried interest on every account against Pavilion's zero carry on primaries.
Pavilion loses. A GroveStreet client owns its own account and sets its own pacing, manager list and geography, which a blind pool with a fixed Japan 30, China 20, Korea 20 and India-SEA-ANZ 30 split cannot match. Fifteen investors holding over US$10bn through 50 accounts since 1998 shows the model holds, and an institution already running one of those accounts will widen it into Asia rather than open a second manager relationship and a second reporting line. Fees there are fully negotiated, so a CalPERS-scale buyer can price below Pavilion's 1 per cent and 10 per cent blended.

Jade Invest
Shanghai. F: the About Us page names offices in Shanghai and London. F: the active director of the UK company gives a correspondence address of Shanghai Tower, 64th Floor, Suite 6404, 501 Yincheng Middle Road, Shanghai, which ties the UK entity to the Shanghai firm. · founded 2005

Not a peer
Jade China Value Partners II, L.P., 2010 vintage, US$65m sold against a US$300m target as at the October 2011 amendment. F: no later SEC filing exists for this or any Jade entity. EDGAR full-text returns exactly two filings naming Jade China Value Partners, the original Form D of 2010-10-19 and the amendment of 2011-10-19. Mainland China only. F: the About Us page says a focus on mainland China, the Strategy page says over a decade of investing in mainland China, and the homepage headline is Investing in China's emerging middle market. This is a China book and it does not carry an Asia label, so it is not a comparator for Pavilion's Japan 30 / China 20 / Korea 20 / India-SEA-ANZ 30 split. It also records on its Background page that China's economic slowdown and prolonged IPO freeze exposed serious weaknesses in GPs' portfolio management capabilities, which is why it left the fund-of-funds model for direct control. That is a useful line for Jodi, because a China manager has put in writing why it stopped backing Chinese GPs blind. · ex-China option no
No named investor found on the public record.
Pavilion wins. Jade China Value Partners II drew 20 investors in 2010 and no successor multi-manager vehicle followed, so those China fund-of-funds slots have sat unfilled for fifteen years. Jade has since told its own market in writing that China's slowdown and IPO freeze exposed serious weaknesses in GPs' portfolio management, which is the argument for diversifying out of a single-country China book. Pavilion answers that with China held to roughly 20% inside a pan-Asia portfolio, a China-exclusion sleeve for anyone who wants less, and Japan at 30% and Korea at 20% as the ballast Jade's investors never had. The firm's own description of its backers as insurance companies and development funds points at DFIs, which publish their fund commitments.
Pavilion loses. An investor that chose Jade wanted concentrated mainland China exposure, and Pavilion's 20% China weight is too thin to replace it. Jade's own history is the counterargument Jodi will meet in the room, because this manager moved its clients from multi-manager exposure into direct control investing and told them why, which argues for co-investment and direct deals over a book that is still 60% primaries at first close. Pavilion's Temasek parent is the harder problem with this audience. A Singapore state-owned shareholder reads as a constraint to an investor whose whole thesis is unconstrained mainland access, and no fee concession on a 1% and 10% blended rate fixes that.
Japanese gatekeepers sweep (Sumitomo Mitsui Trust, SMBC, Nomura, Tokio Marine, Daiwa, Mizuho, MUFG, Norinchukin, Japan Post, Shinsei, Development Bank of Japan and peers)
Tokyo
Ownership and background
Various. The material point is that Japan's private-equity gatekeeping sits inside the trust banks, chiefly Mitsubishi UFJ Trust and Banking and Sumitomo Mitsui Trust, and inside two bank-owned specialists, Alternative Investment Capital for SMBC and Sumitomo Mitsui Trust Investment for the Sumitomo Mitsui Trust group.
Overlap with Pavilion: global FoF with Asia sleeve
⚫
Not a peer
SUMISEI-ALM Investment Limited Partnership, announced 17 January 2025, is the newest Japanese multi-manager PE vehicle found. Its stated investment target is domestic buyout and venture funds.
Japan domestic and global. Asia as a region does not appear as a product category at any of the thirteen institutions checked.
China: Not found. No Japanese institution checked publishes a China policy for private equity, because none of them sells an Asia product that would need one. · ex-China option not found
No named investor found on the public record.
Pavilion wins. The negative is the sales argument. A Japanese corporate pension, life insurer or bank that wants diversified Asian private equity cannot buy it from a Japanese house today, because the trust banks sell domestic buyout and venture fund-of-funds and buy global exposure from Hamilton Lane. GPIF's own private equity book is 62 percent North America and 5 percent Japan at 31 March 2026, with Asia not broken out at all. Pavilion's Japan 30, China 20, Korea 20 and India, Southeast Asia and Australia 30 split at 1 percent and 10 percent blended fills a shelf that has been empty in Tokyo since AIC's Asia fund closed in 2011.
Pavilion loses. An empty shelf in Japan may reflect demand rather than supply. Japanese institutions have had fifteen years to ask a trust bank for an Asia fund-of-funds and the product does not exist, which points to home bias, currency hedging cost and a preference for North America. Pavilion also has to sell through the gatekeepers rather than around them, because Mitsubishi UFJ Trust and Sumitomo Mitsui Trust sit between the money and the manager, and both have an economic reason to prefer a mandate they run themselves. Pavilion's Temasek ownership is a liability in this channel, since a Singapore state holding company asking Japanese pension trustees for a US$50m ticket into a first-time fund invites a governance conversation before an investment one.

Pavilion wins. Korea is 20% of Pavilion's stated geography and the domestic machinery there does not compete for the same allocation, because KVIC's money is appropriated rather than raised. Pavilion wins. Where one Asia bucket funds both, Pavilion's 12+1+1 term, 8% preferred return and direct secondaries give an allocator a claim on the illiquidity premium that a hedge fund-of-funds cannot produce. Pavilion should not spend meeting time on this name as a competitor and should treat Penjing's client list as a source of Asia-comfortable allocators who have never bought Asia private equity.
Pavilion loses. An allocator that already covers Asia alternatives through Penjing's liquid book may see no reason to accept a twelve-year lock-up and a US$50m minimum ticket for the same geographic exposure. On fees the comparison is also unhelpful, because a hedge fund-of-funds at roughly 1% and 10% charges what Pavilion charges while giving quarterly liquidity.

Andrew Junkin
Virginia Retirement System
🔴
partly wrong
Andrew Junkin is CIO. Joined January 2023. Before that 2 years as CIO of Rhode Island and 15 years at Wilshire.VRS news release published 2026-09-17: 'Andrew Junkin, VRS' chief investment officer, said this year's results reflect an investment strategy rooted in diversification with a disciplined approach to risk management.' The live VRS leadership page read 2026-09-19 lists 'Andrew Junkin / Chief Investment Officer / ajunkin@varetire.org'. Seat is current. The join date is wrong: the VRS/PRNewswire appointment release of 2022-05-25 states 'Junkin will begin serving in the CIO role in September' and separately that 'current CIO Ron Schmitz will retire effective January 2023'. January 2023 is the predecessor's retirement date, not Junkin's start. Prior-career facts check out: the same release says 'he was with Wilshire Consulting for 15 years and served as president for the last five years,' and Rhode Island's own release of 2020-06-23 says he was hired 'effective June 22, 2020,' giving a Rhode Island tenure of about two years and three months before he left for VRS in September 2022.
as of 2026-09-17
Andrew Junkin is Chief Investment Officer of the Virginia Retirement System. He started in September 2022 (not January 2023, which was predecessor Ron Schmitz's retirement date). Before VRS he was CIO of Rhode Island for about two years (June 2020 to September 2022) and spent 15 years at Wilshire Consulting, the last five as president.

Wes Bradle
Florida State Board of Administration
🔴
partly wrong
Wes Bradle is a private-equity director covering co-investments and secondaries.The ILPA member-profile page, live and fetched 2026-09-19 (identical content captured by the Wayback Machine on 2026-06-06), heads the page: "Wesley Bradle / Senior Portfolio Manager / Head of PE Co-Investments / Florida State Board of Administration", and the bio reads "Wes Bradle is a Senior Portfolio Manager and Head of PE Co-Investments at the Florida State Board of Administration (FSBA). Mr. Bradle has sourced more than 300 co-investment opportunities since August 2023. He previously monitored 17 PE firms and 50 funds that represented more than $3.8 billion in committed capital. Prior to FSBA, he was an Investment Officer in Private Equity at CalPERS." WHAT IS RIGHT: the institution, private equity, and co-investment coverage. WHAT IS WRONG: his title is Senior Portfolio Manager / Head of PE Co-Investments, not "director" - the SBA org chart uses "Senior Investment Officer", "Senior Portfolio Manager" and "Portfolio Manager" for its investment ranks. WHAT IS UNVERIFIED: secondaries. The bio's only secondaries reference is that he has spoken at the "Carmo Private Markets Secondaries" conference, which is a speaking venue, not a stated remit; the SBA's March 2026 IAC private-equity appendix does track Secondaries and Co-Investments as separate sub-strategies but never names who covers them. He is not named in the SBA's own March 2026 IAC materials or its org chart, which go down only to John Bradley, Senior Investment Officer - Private Equity.

Norway · Sovereign wealth fund manager, a separate unit of the Norwegian central bank
🟢
strength 5
no - barred by the mandate in force in 2026. FOR-2010-11-08-1414 section 2-1 first paragraph letter a confines the equity portfolio to listed shares, securities equivalent to listed shares, depositary receipts and unlisted companies whose board has stated an intention to seek a listing. Letters c and d open unlisted investment only for real estate and renewable energy infrastructure. Letter e permits fund units only where they connect naturally to those portfolios. A pan-Asia private-equity fund-of-funds fits none of them.
Typical ticket: Not applicable. SPU makes no private-equity fund commitments.

It discussed 'minimumnormen private equity' and 'make-buy-ally (MBA)-analyses van de beleggingscategorieen' in 2025. The PGGM PE Investment Committee meets on deal flow rather than a published calendar.

↑ Jump menu📩What I still need from you

Answers to the first two would change the shape of the outreach more than any further research on my side.

📊 1The Preqin or Dakota investor export for the other fund-of-funds

You sent the Axiom Asia investor export on 14 September and it produced 112 named LPs. The same export for HarbourVest Asia Pacific, Pantheon Asia, LGT Crown Asia-Pacific and Asia Alternatives would turn the public-record list below into a complete one. Public filings catch US public pensions, UK LGPS funds and Australian super funds. They miss endowments, family offices, insurers and every Asian LP.

🤝 2Who you and the Pavilion team already know

The relationship column across all 353 institutions is empty because it is the one thing no public source holds. A name, a firm and how warm it is, even on 30 of them, reorders the whole call list.

📄 3The rest of the Pavilion materials

Six slides is the strategy. The review needs the track record by fund and vintage with net IRR, TVPI and DPI, the underlying GP list, the team page with tenure, and the fund terms document. Every serious LP asks for those four in the first meeting.

💳 4Whether US$50m is a floor or a preference

Eleven of the nineteen Canadians here cannot write US$50m to one fund. If Pavilion will take US$20m to US$25m, the addressable Canadian list roughly doubles and several mid-size US and European names come back into range.

🇨🇳 5How the China-exclusion sleeve actually works

A side letter, a parallel vehicle or a separate close. Several of the institutions here have a written China policy, and the answer decides whether they can look at the fund at all.

🏗️ 6Whether Temasek is anchoring, and with how much

Every LP will ask inside the first ten minutes. A number with a source settles it. Silence reads as no.

🗑️ 7Pavilion's own coverage list and the do-not-contact names

Which institutions Pavilion has already approached, which are re-ups from earlier Pavilion funds, and who is off limits. Without it a share of this outreach duplicates the GP.

📞 8Two or three reference LPs who will take a call

Named existing Pavilion investors willing to speak to a prospect. In a first-time fund on a new brand this does more work than any slide.

🌎 9Which geographies are actually in your mandate

The work so far covers the US, Canada, the UK, the Nordics and continental Europe. Whether Australia, Japan, Korea, the Middle East and the Swiss and German family-office channel are yours or the GP's decides where the next sweep goes.

🗑 10Your CRM export

Column names and record IDs, so the next deliverable imports into your system instead of being retyped.

Sources are linked in every cell and in full in the CSV files. The F: and I: notation is defined in the key above the first table. Where the record is silent the cell says not found rather than guessing.
